mr president, commissioner, your proposal is bad, it is very bad indeed. it is good that there is a parliament, a parliament which is a co-legislator and is on an equal footing with the council. with our proposal we can continue to call cohesion policy cohesion policy, with your proposal we could not call it cohesion policy. how could we accept a 45% cut in the cohesion fund, the fund which is intended precisely for the poorest member states, those which have less than 90% of gross national income? the cohesion and solidarity of the european union is a matrix which we cannot lose, which we must at least maintain in terms of amounts. and with this proposal of ours, with the maintenance of the amounts, with the maintenance of the cofinancing rates, which are essential for the poorer regions, we can tell the citizens of the european union that social, economic and territorial cohesion will continue and thanks to parliament which will now negotiate with the member states. and here is a question for the commissioner: which member states are actually cutting back on cohesion policy? parliament has already said here, and will vote, that it will not accept cuts in this policy.
in writing. the so-called macroeconomic conditionality has been and is at the basis of blackmail and the threat of sanctions to states, as was done to portugal in 2016, with the prospect of suspending the structural and investment funds due to the failure to meet the deficit and debt targets imposed by the eu. but that is not the only negative aspect of this proposal. these common provisions continue to link cohesion policy to other forms of conditionality, stemming from the european semester, its instruments (country recommendations, national reform plans) and the neoliberal agenda of the european union. the decision-making process on priorities for the implementation of the funds decisions that are increasingly outside the institutions of national sovereignty and the specific development needs of each country and a thematic concentration on the strategic objectives of the eu, defined in the interests of its major powers, but independent of national strategic priorities and objectives, are maintained. the overall amounts foreseen for the structural and investment funds are also still insufficient. it is not enough to keep 'cohesion' funds at the same level as the current budget, they must be reinforced to counteract the dynamics of economic and social divergence prevailing within the eu.

mr president, proper health technology assessment (hta) can be the unbiased tipping point for the game-changing technologies available for patients. this is a matter of supranational interest for european citizens and cannot be weighed only by economic factors. assessments, pricing and reimbursement can be set as a touchstone for the sustainability of health systems. so far, this is the only tool able to ensure that citizens have affordable and effective treatments vis-à-vis the values demanded by profit-making manufacturers. however, the reality shows that hta is fragmented throughout europe. in fact, by reaching different outcomes, these assessments leave citizens and health professionals with different spans of choice, according to their member states. here, you can also count on us to fight for equal opportunities. this proposal relies on the collaboration of member states but still leaves room for national decisions. by centralising the clinical assessment we can avoid duplication, save resources and, above all, create a european union with less asymmetry in access to health care. this proposal is only effective with the combined efforts of the national regulatory agencies and i greatly salute the integration of patient representatives in the assessment procedure. this will make hta involved from mere farmer ecological data on to a more realistic evaluation. that weights the contributions to the patients’ quality of life. but for the assessments to be beyond any doubt, they need to be as transparent as possible. this is the only way to build confidence in the health technology chain of value. i hope this regulation will bring us closer to the united, safe and healthy europe we all long for.

territory of several member states. let me start with the basic yet fundamental premise. the european union has consistently voiced, and will continue to voice, our strong unequivocal condemnation of terrorism in all its forms and manifestations committed by whomsoever, wherever and for whatever purposes. it is then clear that in order to preserve our own european security, we cannot accept iranian or any other country’s hostile activities on european soil, no matter the justification. there is complete solidarity between the european union and all eu member states on this issue, and this solidarity will continue. whenever there have been reports of such activities, we have been in prompt contact with the authorities of the member state concerned to offer our full support and solidarity. for example, in the case of denmark at the end of last october, hr / vp mogherini discussed the situation with the danish foreign minister on the very same day that the case was made public. you will also be aware that the foreign affairs council had two significant discussions on iran in the final months of 2018. at the foreign affairs council in november, the danish foreign minister anders samuelsen provided an update and member states underlined their solidarity and willingness to agree on measures to address this and other recent incidents on european territory. one month later in december, the foreign affairs council again discussed iran. ministers agreed on the principle of adoption conclusions, which would reaffirm the commitment to the joint comprehensive plan of action (jcpoa), while making clear that it would not prevent the eu from raising concerns about iranian activities. this solidarity at the council led to the addition of two iranian individuals and the directorate for internal security of the iranian ministry for intelligence and security to the eu counter-terrorism list. the council adopted the list on 9 january this year. persons, groups and entities on this list are subject to the freezing of their funds and other financial assets, and european union operators are prohibited from making funds and economic resources available to them. our discussions on how we should collectively respond to threats to our security, whatever their provenance, did not end on that day, as the conclusions adopted on 4 february repeated that the european union will continue to demonstrate unity and solidarity in this area. i would like to close these introductory remarks with three points. first, far from responding to policies of iran with which we disagree, we have a number of restrictive measures in place against iranian individuals and entities for reasons including their violation of human rights, support for terrorism and support for the assad regime. secondly, we should resist the temptation of viewing any country as solely a perpetrator of terrorism. iran too has been a victim of terror attacks and consistency demands that we have also condemned, as in the case of the attacks in ahvaz in september last year, which claimed the lives of at least 25 individuals. finally, our vigorous response to these actions, while necessary, should not affect the eu’s continuing commitment to the full and effective implementation of the jcpoa whose preservation is in the security interests of the eu and the international community. thank you for your attention and i look forward to our discussion.

member of the commission. – mr president, in may of last year the commission adopted a communication on a europe that protects clean air for all. in this communication, we outlined the eu clean air policy approach, provided examples of measures to reduce air pollution in the transport, power and heat energy industry, and also in the agricultural sectors. we also point to initiatives taken to step-up cooperation with member states, with regions, and also with cities. against this background let me reply to your five questions. first on the first question, the commission has been taking decisive action where it sees persistent exceedances above air quality limit values. it is currently pursuing 30 infringement procedures against 20 member states for exceedances of particulate matter, nitrogen dioxide or sulphur dioxide limit values. in two cases, the court of justice has already ruled against the member states. the commission will continue to actively manage the infringement process as fast as possible, including referrals to the court where and when warranted. with regards to your second question, as you know, the commission is carrying out a fitness check of the ambient air quality directives. we initiated this in 2017 and will complete it in the second half of this year. its findings will be used to inform further reflections on whether these directives continue to provide the appropriate legislative framework to ensure protection from adverse impacts on, and risks to, human health and the environment. however, as the analysis is not yet over, it would be premature to anticipate now the conclusions the commission might draw. referring to question three, european union funding under various financial streams has been made available and successfully used by member states to address the air quality challenge. in the current programing period – that is 2014 to 2020 – eur 1.8 billion have been directly allocated to support air quality measures under the european structural and investment fund. in addition, further indirect contributions beneficial to clean air come from investments in the low-carbon economy and environmental protection and resource efficiency, or in the transport network infrastructure. more specifically, to support regions and cities, in the urban agenda for the eu there have been innovative actions to further facilitate addressing air pollution in urban areas where transport is one of the main sources of air pollution. looking ahead for the period post-2020, the commission has proposed under the cohesion policy a particular emphasis on a green low carbon europe including a specific result indicator on population benefiting from measures for air quality. on question number four, the agricultural sector is an important source of ammonia emissions, which in turn are a key precursor to particulate matter. measures to mitigate these emissions include agronomic measures, livestock measures, or energy production measures. they are already available, technically and economical viable, and should be applied more widely. according to the commission's proposal for a new common agricultural policy after 2020, one of the impact indicators to assess the performance of this policy explicitly refers to the ammonia emissions from agriculture. to be more specific, a result indicator will measure the share of agricultural land under commitments to reduce ammonia emissions. on the fifth question, eu legislation sets objectives for ambient air quality, taking into account the relevant scientific information provided via the world health organisation, which is the world’s leading authority on health matters. their air quality guidelines establish the most authoritative, scientific basis to date. the world health organisation keeps the scientific basis under regular review, including ultra-fine particles and black carbon. in addition, it is currently working towards an update of these air quality guidelines. the commission is following this work very closely.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, i would like to thank the commissioner for his excellent replies. according to the european academy of allergy and clinical immunology, more than 100 million europeans suffer from allergic rhinitis and 70 million from asthma. also, the european respiratory society has warned that even short-term increases in air pollution have been associated with respiratory symptoms and temporary decreases in lung function. in projections by the oecd, air pollution will be the biggest environmental cause of premature deaths by 2050. millions of europeans live in areas where simply breathing the air around them is damaging to their health, and these effects should not be underestimated. air pollution can reduced lifespans and cause serious heart and lung disease. the pollutants of most concern for human health across europe are airborne particulate matter, nitrogen dioxide and ground-level ozone. these pollutants increase the death rate, especially in sensitive population groups such as the elderly and those suffering from respiratory diseases. despite these health impacts, current eu legislation permits levels of damaging pollutants higher than those recommended by the world health organisation. what still needs to be done? member states should reinforce their efforts to tackle sources of pollution from all sectors. states also need to improve cooperation on cross—border pollution, make air quality an integral part of their policies, and ensure that the correct level of governance – national, regional or municipal – is equipped to tackle sources of pollution. i recently hosted an exhibition in parliament celebrating lisbon as 2020 european green capital, a designation for which one of the 12 criteria evaluated was air quality. in the past 10 years, lisbon has shown a significant decrease in emissions from most pollutants, resulting in a significant improvement in air quality. by limiting polluting vehicles in the city, without reducing mobility, and by planning green corridors that act as a ventilation system, lisbon was able to achieve its aim on air quality. commissioner, we need more examples like lisbon and we need national strategies for air quality.
member of the commission. – mr president, first i would agree on the sensitive and at the same time important issue of the inclusion of dual eu/third-country nationals. for the commission it is vital to include in the ecris—tcn system all third-country nationals, including those who are also eu nationals, otherwise we would leave an important loophole and undermine the objective of the proposal. what we have to avoid is that criminals play off different nationalities to hide possible previous convictions. we recognise that this establishes a different approach between eu citizens who only have an eu nationality and those also holding the nationality of a third country. however, we consider this is justified by the fact that the latter can use either of these nationalities when confronted with a prosecution, or in other cases where their previous criminal record might hamper them. for example, if the persons concerned had already been convicted for a sexual offence under their tcn nationality, they might use only their eu nationality when applying for a position involving working with children in another member state. excluding persons with eu/third-country nationality would mean that the identity data of certain persons with a criminal record, who hold the nationality of a third country, would not be stored in the ecris—tcn system. i would like to thank you for your interventions. i would like to encourage you to vote in favour of the compromise texts of both proposals. the new system for the exchange of criminal records is an important contribution to the security union and to the european justice area.
in writing. when necessary and this is an area where one should not go beyond what is necessary , the transmission of information on the criminal records of citizens should be carried out on a bilateral cooperation basis, to be established between the states involved. this european system goes further, entering a recognizably sensitive area, because it is associated with individual rights, freedoms and guarantees, and is included in the constitutional acquis of some member states. going beyond what is necessary, this system creates problems. it emphasises the attempt to move towards the federalisation of justice. by placing data on citizens of the member states in the hands of supranational institutions which are not exactly benchmarks for the safeguarding of individual rights and freedoms. no clear guarantees as to the protection of this data. all this in the context of a security drift, driven by a bold and dubious definition of terrorism, and immigration policies aimed at punishing secondary movements and facilitating expulsion. another example of how the views and conceptions of the far right are making their way through the hands of the right and social democracy.
madam president, more and more of our lives are being lived and shared online. that's why our personal data is now such a valuable commodity that companies and governments want to collect, exploit or access it. that's also why there are so many criminals who want to steal them. recent scandals like facebook and cambridge analytica are the most real demonstration of this. we need the means to deal with these threats, but we also need to ensure that respect for human rights is at the heart of the work of organisations that aim to combat cybercrime. so we cannot ignore the essential role that people have to play in this struggle. we must not ignore the need for citizens to be empowered to make more informed decisions about their data, how to manage it and how to keep it safe. we need bodies like enisa, but we also need all the authorities and people to be aware of their role in this fight and never, never, cross the line of human rights, however desirable the profits.
madam president, cybersecurity is today, as all the facts show, one of the fundamental keys to ensuring the competitiveness of the european economy, protecting its citizens from the threats posed by new technologies used illegally and developing free democratic processes of choice and representation. it therefore makes sense to strengthen enisa's capacity and potential by increasing the resilience of the european space against cyber-attacks and by creating enhanced prevention, detection and response competences and capabilities. but it is also essential to implement a system of certification of products and systems. to achieve this requires cooperation and coordination. national knowledge and development centres will receive a strong boost from horizon europe and digital europe. the certification network should build on these competences and operate in an articulation model capable of ensuring, in a distributed and aggregated manner, the integrity and resilience of procedures and networks and the quality of information. it is not an easy challenge, but it is a priority we must meet to ensure the european union's strategic autonomy in the new digital society.
mr president, commissioner, i should like to begin by congratulating our colleague, professor paolo de castro, on the outcome of the negotiations, which is the result of his clear commitment to the defence of farmers. this directive is an important step in strengthening the bargaining position of farmers and increasing their protection against abusive commercial practices. it will introduce a common minimum level of protection throughout the european union in a harmonised way, in addition to the provisions already adopted in some countries, such as portugal. this will certainly contribute to fairer prices for producers. we must also emphasize here, among other things, the decisive role played by the european parliament in improving the commission's initial proposal, contributing in particular to a significant increase in the number of practices covered and the inclusion of buyers outside the european union.
mr president, mr commissioner, the end of the mandate is approaching and now more than ever our fellow citizens are looking to us for a sort of balance sheet to show them the importance for their lives of what we have done here. the directive on combating unfair trading practices in the agri-food supply chain is a good example of how we stand up for our farmers and, by extension, for all europeans. despite years of delay, we have had the will and support of all parties in this mandate and we have reached consensus quite quickly on a directive which will allow greater stability and income security for our farmers and will punish all those who abuse their dominant position over the other links in the food chain. it is therefore a great day for european agriculture, which will see unfair commercial practices enshrined in law, such as unilateral contract changes, the attempt to blame farmers for the economic losses of distribution, the payment of perishable products in a period of more than 30 days, the imposition of discount schemes or penalties without prior agreement, among many others. the possibility for a producer organisation to make a complaint on behalf of one of its members is also a great step towards putting an end to expressions of apprehension, especially in small markets or where they accepted the conditions that they would be supplied or would no longer be supplied by that entity. i would like to conclude, mr president, by saying that i am convinced that this european parliament has left the agricultural sector a little more protected than it found it. it is a very happy day for european agriculture.
mr president, portuguese farmers are now left with less than 20% of the price consumers pay for their products. we all know the predatory role of the retail sector in agriculture. we are all familiar with the practices of crushing the prices paid to the producer. it is intolerable and regrettable that the european union has done nothing in these five years. it is now coming out with this directive on the eve of elections and, needless to say, better late than never. there are certainly positive aspects to this proposal, but it does not touch on the fundamental issue. their mechanisms are insufficient to guarantee this very simple thing: that prices cover production costs and guarantee a decent standard of living for our farmers. this proposal is certainly a step forward, but it falls far short of what is needed.

mr president, the security of the citizens and the financial system of the european union must be protected against the illegal instruments that enable the financing of terrorism and money laundering. the union has the capacity to draw up a list of third countries on the basis of the risk identified through suspicious capital flows between shell companies and opaque structures used to conceal the true beneficiaries of a transaction, including for the purposes of tax evasion. based on intelligence provided by europol and the financial action task force, we were able to tighten the assessment mesh and increase the list of suspect countries. it must be understood that with this list we are not applying any economic sanctions or making any diplomatic boycott. it's the opposite. it is therefore not understandable that the council should refuse to accept the list of countries at risk proposed by the commission. the union must maintain its commitment to intervene in countries with strategic deficiencies to prevent dirty money from being used to finance corruption, organised crime and terrorism. of course, no country is happy to be on this blacklist and tries, by all means, including through bilateral talks, to put together arguments to justify its exclusion. my dear colleagues, we have agreed: we know how insidious it can be for our union to have corruption, organised crime and terrorism penetrate the european territory. there are matters on which europe must close ranks and act with one voice, and in this case it is the purest common sense that prevention is better than cure.
the rapporteur. - mr president, commissioner, minister, ladies and gentlemen, i cannot help recalling that schengen and freedom of movement are the successes of european integration which europeans value most. but this freedom of movement requires measures to ensure the security of our common borders and cooperation between police forces within our territory. it also requires a common visa policy, that is, a european approach to those who cross our borders legally, whether to visit, study or work. the visa information system, the vis, is the most visible example of this policy. currently this system is limited to tourist entry, so-called short-stay visas. in 2017 alone, some 16 million visas were issued. of course, it is not just tourists who enter schengen. in 2017, one million workers entered the european union, more than 800,000 people came to join their families and more than half a million came to study. these people, although also benefiting from free movement, are not registered in any european database. more worryingly, each member state decides which security controls it is subject to. there is also a final category of people, the big investors, the so-called gold visas, which are already issued by 20 member states. these are also currently outside the vis and are not subject to any security controls against other union information systems such as the schengen information system. with this reform of the vis we intend to change that. in addition, this reform will only make sense if long-term visas and residence permits, including gold visas, are registered in the vis. that is why i am surprised and concerned to see that the s&d group has changed its mind in the last month. when we voted on this report in the committee, they supported the measure, now they seem to want to follow the greens and the gue. ladies and gentlemen, let us be clear: the current distinction between short-term visas and long-term documents makes no sense. anyone applying for a visa to enter the european union must be subject to the same security checks throughout europe. tourists who visit us and who normally stay on our territory for a period of well under 90 days are subject to european security checks. but people who decide to settle more permanently in our territory or acquire residence through investments, i am talking about the gold visas, are not subject to even remotely similar controls. that is why, with this reform, we are first of all seeking to ensure that all visas, whatever their duration, are automatically preceded by adequate security checks, in full respect of fundamental rights, the principles of necessity and proportionality, but ensuring that a russian investor who is the subject of a european arrest warrant does not end up with a gold visa in romania. these checks shall be carried out systematically and thoroughly before the issuing of visas. the commission has already proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of the euro against counterfeiting. it is precisely for security reasons that the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs of this parliament has taken the view that fingerprints should be taken from the age of six. only then can we better protect our children, whether it's against identity fraud, kidnapping or trafficking. every year 15,000 people are trafficked in europe, of whom at least 3,000 are children. we have a duty to fight this scourge and through this reform we are doing so. mr president, with this strengthened vis we will make it easier to issue visas, prevent so-called visa shopping, visa fraud and illegal immigration, we will increase scrutiny of gold visas and better protect our children. finally, we have taken another important step towards an area of free movement with justice and security. we are one step closer to a true citizens' europe.
rapporteur. – mr president, i would like to thank my colleagues for their contributions to the european people’s party (epp) and to myself. to keep the scope of the system is crucial. without it, this reform loses much of its value. we will not be serving european citizens well if we take that out. as citizens’ representatives, we have the obligation to defend their best interests and we are accountable before them for the votes that we cast in this house. it is true and evident that every opinion is legitimate, but rest assured, those who think that golden visas should not be subject to greater scrutiny and those who do not want to better protect children will have to explain their actions to their voters. i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs for their very constructive spirit. i also want to extend my gratitude to your staff, the secretary of this house and the services of the european commission. you have all been essential to this outcome.
on behalf of the ppe group. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would first like to thank my colleague, tanja fajon, for the excellent cooperation we have had and to underline that the management of our external borders remains and will continue to be a key issue for the european union, whether through its security component or visa policy. that is why, as we plan for the next decade, this fund cannot fail to reflect what we have learned from the more recent past. first, more money: in border management, member states remain the main actors, which is why, together with a reinforcement of the european border and coast guard agency, we have increased the available funds by five times; it's about ten billion dollars that will go to help secure our borders and at the same time promote legal entry into the territory. secondly, more flexibility: the recent past has shown that we need greater solidarity and greater speed in emergency situations; the union cannot be held hostage to momentary agreements to come to the aid of member states in difficulty. that is why we propose greater flexibility in the management of the fund in exceptional and urgent cases. third, greater justice: at our initiative, the so-called cohesion countries, such as portugal, will have 85% instead of 75% of funding; the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the allocation of the funds for the period from 1 january 1997 to 31 december 2000 to the member states. finally, i have often said in this house that there are no measures that can solve all the problems at once. what we must and can do is set priorities and work to improve. this is another decisive step towards protecting our borders. let's keep working.
the withdrawal of the united kingdom from the european union is a legally protected right. in the face of the results of the referendum held in that member state and the decision taken by its government to trigger the withdrawal procedures, the eu participated unitedly in a negotiating process based on safeguarding its values and protecting its citizens and businesses without harassing the citizens, institutions or civil society of the united kingdom. throughout the process, the peoples of europe have made clear their respect for the will of the british people, and at the same time, they have noted the desire to achieve a good deal or to maintain the territorial integrity of the union. the impact assessment of a possible no-deal exit has revealed that this scenario, if it occurs, will be highly pernicious for both parties. it is with this assumption that the european negotiators tried to the last moment to respond with openness and common sense to the requests of the british negotiators. the british parliament has chosen to reject the proposed agreement outright. the european union in general and parliament in particular must maintain an attitude of firmness in values and openness to solutions in order to defend the interests of all its citizens.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the debate in this parliament on the guidelines for economic and budgetary policy is always an important part of the european semester, but it is even more so in the present context of enormous risks and uncertainties and of slowing economic growth. the final version of the report on which we are voting this afternoon is, fortunately, quite different from the initial version. many of the proposals put forward by my group have been accepted and i am therefore pleased to say that the final version of this report will have the support of the socialist group. i would therefore like to thank the rapporteur, our colleague tom vandenkendelaere, for the excellent cooperation we have had, which has enabled us to achieve this good result. the main message from this parliament is that european economic and budgetary policy needs to take a step forward to respond to the slowdown in the economy. we need concerted action for more sustainable and inclusive growth, we need to overcome the public and private investment gap and we need to ensure that member states with budgetary surpluses do more to grow the european economy by increasing wages and investment. responsibility for the management of public accounts is of course still necessary, but it must be compatible with investment in job creation, the fight against inequalities, the promotion of convergence, social inclusion, the energy transition, the fight against climate change and the objectives of sustainable development. it is this other view of investment policies and the idea of structural reforms that we need to make the european pillar of social rights effective. these are the fundamental elements of the economic and budgetary strategy that europe needs and that is why we shall vote in favour of this report.
the reform of the european stability mechanism is one of the most important pillars of the process of reforming economic and monetary union, a process necessary to address the enormous shortcomings in the architecture of the euro that the financial crisis has so dramatically revealed. with this report, parliament is making a very timely contribution to this process and i would therefore like to thank the rapporteur, our colleague mr vladimir manka, for the excellent cooperation we have had in the last few months of work. i also thank the shadow rapporteurs from the various groups for the excellent spirit of cooperation which has enabled us to reach what i believe to be a good result. in this report parliament advocates a two-step strategy: the european parliament reaffirms its clear view that the european stability mechanism should be fully integrated into european law so that it can become a genuine institution of the european union, governed in accordance with the community method and under the democratic control of the european parliament. if this is not possible immediately, we would like to see progress in the reform of the european stability mechanism through a revision of the intergovernmental treaty which created it. we did not think it appropriate, unlike the commission, to change the name of this institution to the european monetary fund because this could create confusion as to the competence to conduct monetary policy, which should continue to be the responsibility of the european central bank, but there are more important things we can and must do: firstly, to strengthen the democratic control of the mechanism by the european parliament. we therefore propose a protocol of cooperation between the two institutions to strengthen dialogue, to strengthen parliamentary scrutiny and we propose more, we propose that the director of the european stability mechanism be elected here in the european parliament. secondly, we advocate strengthening the role of the mechanism in promoting financial stability, in responding to economic and financial shocks and, therefore, also by reviewing its tasks and powers, without prejudice to the tasks of macroeconomic surveillance which should continue to be the responsibility of the european commission. finally, greater autonomy vis-à-vis the international monetary fund must be achieved and the mechanism should be given powers to make the single resolution fund operational and thus contribute to the completion of the banking union. we all know that the agenda for reforming the euro goes beyond this, it requires budgetary capacity, it requires many other things, but the reform of the european stability mechanism is also an important step and we hope that now the council will listen to the proposals and suggestions of the european parliament so that we can move in the right direction.
mr president, our debate confirms that this parliament wants the reform of the european stability mechanism to help make the single resolution fund operational in order to move towards the completion of the banking union as envisaged. but we want much more, we want a genuinely european stability mechanism, which is integrated into european law and subject to the control of this parliament. we want it to have more autonomy vis-à-vis the imf and therefore to turn the page on the troika. we want it to do more to support the states and to learn all the lessons of past assistance programmes, which also means not failing to make a serious assessment of the social impact of the measures to be included in future assistance programmes. these are the messages of the interim report which we are going to adopt, and we now hope that the council and the commission will listen to this parliament which represents the european citizens.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. built in the context of the single market and the european aviation strategy, it is part of the eu's vision of promoting concentration in the sector, facilitating the creation of european aviation monopolies. a strategy which has contributed to the degradation or destruction of flag carriers, which has fuelled the privatisation processes of the sector, whether of airlines, infrastructure or services such as ground handling. this process has resulted in heavy losses for the countries, but also for workers, whose labour rights have been significantly reduced and precariousness in the sector has increased. the proposal is based on a dichotomy between eu and non-eu companies in the fight for the single market. it seeks to interfere with the organization of the sector in foreign countries by creating conditionalities on the authorization to operate of companies that have state subsidies. nothing new about that. they also interfere with national strategies for the aviation sector. the consequences of this competition between companies based in the eu and the single market are omitted in order to encourage the so-called low-cost companies that add up profits at the expense of the degradation of the quality of service, the assault on workers' rights, often in violation of labour laws. these are all the more reason to reject this proposal.
i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. the european union and the european parliament insist on manipulating facts and adding to the trump administration's interventionist strategy of interference and destabilization throughout latin america. you know that the reform of social security proposed in nicaragua was intended to ensure its sustainability and at the same time to avoid the implementation of the neoliberal recommendations proposed by the imf. they know that the wave of protests, regimenting the legitimate expectations and desires of the population, was manipulated and orchestrated by sectors of the most reactionary right, deliberately provoking violent clashes and deaths. they know that this wave of violence was directed not only against the authorities and the government, but against members of the sandinista front who were attacked, kidnapped and murdered. they are aware of the government's initiative to launch a broad dialogue to put an end to the clashes promoted by the opposition and to find peaceful solutions. they know that the united states government is imposing sanctions and threatening nicaragua with an economic veto, preventing access to credits from international financial institutions in violation of the united nations charter and international law. it is because you know all this that you have nothing but lies to attack the peoples and progressive forces of latin america, such as in nicaragua, who insist on asserting the sovereignty and independence of their country, the right to self-determination, and to freely determine the course of the political, economic, social and cultural development of their countries. mr president, democracy is not a model of political organisation independent of the cultural context and history of peoples. there are, however, principles which are an absolute condition for the structuring of a democratic regime. these principles and values underlie our project, commissioner. and so we have in our identity matrix to promote these principles in our territory and in the world. these are principles based on respect for the rule of law, human rights and the identity of peoples. it is with these principles, while others seek the goods and resources in venezuela, that we seek to give back to the venezuelan people the voice and the choice in free elections and it is these principles also that must continue to guide our attitude towards the authoritarian regime in nicaragua. this is the time to use the mechanisms at our disposal under international law to stand with the nicaraguan people. for the sake of democracy and the dignity of the people, we must continue to act with determination and courage, and the resolution we shall be adopting in this house tomorrow is proof of that.
mr president, commissioner, i belong to a generation which, in its adolescence, followed the sandinista revolution in nicaragua with great enthusiasm and great hope. i remember well that every day we waited for news from that country and i remember well the days when we celebrated with great joy the fall of the dictator somoza and the victory of the sandinista revolutionaries. and yet this regime has failed its people, has failed to live up to its historical responsibilities. and as is often the case with regimes that degenerate, it now resorts to disinformation, intimidation and the invention of external enemies to serve as scapegoats for its own failures. like the bolivarian regime in venezuela, the sandinista regime has become a gross distortion of democratic values and the founding values of the democratic left. instead of equality, poverty. instead of freedom, repression of civil society and persecution of political opponents. it is precisely in the name of the values of the democratic left, and i am a socialist, that we must condemn in the strongest possible terms what is happening in nicaragua today. and this resolution does just that. it is a hard and informed motion, which does not shy away from any of the flagrant violations of human rights and the rule of law that are taking place in that country today. it clearly calls for sanctions against members of the government and calls for the suspension of nicaragua's participation in the eu-central america association agreement. and all this in the name of one word: democracy. it is in the name of this word that a democratic revolution was once made and today we can say that this revolution has been a revolution betrayed in recent years.

vice-president of the commission. – mr president, i would like to thank the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs for drawing attention to this matter. the commission is concerned about the high rates of discrimination and the manifestations of hatred reported to be seriously affecting people of african descent and black people in europe, as recently shown by fundamental rights agency research. preventing and fighting afrophobia and other forms of racism are fully embedded in the commission’s policies against discrimination and racism, which remain a clear priority for us. eu action in this area comprises a variety of measures and tools, including the continuing monitoring of the transposition and implementation of relevant eu legislation and, in particular, the race equality directive and the framework decision on combating racism. in the past years, we have been working hard to provide national authorities with guidance on how to ensure the effective implementation of legislation. we have focused on data on equality and hate crime, victim support and improving investigation into, and the prosecution of, hate speech and hate crime. last november, we published a new guidance note on the practical application of the eu framework decision on combating racism, which will serve as a useful tool to prompt and assist national law-enforcement and judicial authorities in better enforcing national legislation on hate crime and hate speech on the ground. flagship policy initiatives, such as a code of conduct on countering illegal hate speech online, and the work on diversity chapters, are also providing a key contribution to concrete progress on the ground. all these horizontal initiatives play a key role in terms of preventing and countering afrophobia and anti-black racism. but, indeed, the recognition of afrophobia and anti-black racism as a specific form of racism is important. following thematic discussion in the eu high level group on combating racism and other forms of intolerance, we published last november a policy paper which provides an overview of the main issues raised by stakeholders on the specific barriers, prejudices and obstacles facing people of african descent and black people across europe. on this basis, targeted measures are identified, in order effectively to address discrimination, exclusion and intolerance directed against those communities. we hope this policy paper can be a useful tool both for policymakers at eu and national level and for ngos and other stakeholders. i look forward to this debate and to the ideas of the european parliament, which will be reflected in the resolution.
on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, commissioner, in the context of combating institutional, personal and internalized racism, the un has declared the period 2015-2024 as the international decade for people of african descent. despite this proclamation, the truth is that in the member states of the european union people of african descent continue to be racially, socially and legally discriminated against, more vulnerable to unemployment and, due to their political underrepresentation in the european institutions, prevented from contributing to the elimination of these forms of discrimination against them. this sad picture is a shame that is seen all over europe, including in my own country, where people of african descent are particularly vulnerable socially and economically and where, unfortunately, reports of police violence based on racial discrimination persist, where numerous portuguese people of african descent continue to be treated as immigrants and immigrant people of african descent see their right to nationality restricted and their rights inherent in it denied to them by institutional racism that persists in the 21st century. it is therefore urgent to create a european union framework for national strategies for the inclusion and social integration of people of african descent, including the promotion of political empowerment, the fight against afrophobia and hate crimes. in the 21st century, we cannot continue to stand by and watch discriminatory treatment of citizens, whether on the basis of colour, religion or sexual preference. europe is, and must remain, an area of freedom, solidarity and equal opportunities for all and non-discrimination in access to employment, pay and even access to health care.

mr president, the debate and decision on the seasonal change of time cannot be dissociated from the geographical, social, cultural and other particularities of each country. the european commission is now proposing, in the name of the so-called proper functioning and harmonization of the single market, to impose an end to the seasonal change of time. we recall that there is no closed position in the scientific community, neither on the modalities in which seasonal time changes should be made, nor on the benefits or harms of the existence of such changes and their implications for the health and well-being of the population. we also recall something that the european commission seems to have forgotten: the establishment of the statutory time is a matter for the member states. for all these reasons, we believe that the existence or non-existence of seasonal time changes must take into account the wishes of the respective population, the opinion of the scientific community and the interest of each country, and we therefore reject any impositions by the european union in this matter.
the commission has also proposed that the council adopt a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a fire. other madam president, commissioner, throughout this parliamentary term the european union has fallen short of its ambition and its obligation to deepen its institutional and political commitment to competitiveness and convergence. however, there have been exceptions: the energy union and the digital union are good examples and that is why we are here today debating this legislation. it is not enough, however, to go forward. the choices and how they ensure the union's leadership in the energy transition, industrial competitiveness and people's access to clean, healthy and affordable electricity need to be taken into account. we also need to ensure the principle of energy efficiency first, ensure a just transition, strengthen transparency and the capacity for market regulation and give priority to production from renewable sources to actually reduce emissions. a single internal market cannot be synonymous with a market without rules. it must be a market where fair competition is possible, in a transparent and coordinated way, without compromising safety and innovation. it is therefore time to shed a new light on the european union and its citizens.
the european union has some of the best consumer protection rules in the world, but the current framework does not allow for a robust and equitable enforcement of this legislation in the union. the risk of breaches of union law affecting the collective interests of consumers is increasing due to economic globalisation and the digitalisation of our economy, and the dieselgate case has exposed the fragility of european consumers in having their rights protected and redressed in large-scale cross-border breaches. more than anything, a basic principle on which the eu is founded is at stake: access to justice for 500 million european consumers by allowing a qualified entity to seek redress on behalf of a group of consumers who have been harmed by an illegal commercial practice. harmonized common principles are established, but national legal traditions are also respected and bad faith litigation is prevented. together with the reform we have made of the regulation on cooperation between national consumer enforcement authorities, the revision of the consumer rights directive and now the collective actions, we have strengthened and sealed the european consumer protection framework.
madam president, the regulation on tyre labelling is an area where rigor and transparency are essential, given that it is a consumer product that separates safety from risk, life from death, sustainable production from unregulated exploitation of resources, environmental quality from worsening pollution and waste accumulation. the principle of transparency and communication, as the commissioner knows, based on the robust recording of the necessary data, must allow this legislation we are looking at to be useful for the sector and, at the same time, to encourage a circular approach to the management of resources. the work of the rapporteurs - which i welcome - will enable us to adopt useful regulations that improve energy regulation, sustainability, safety, defending consumers, without jeopardising the competitiveness of european industry. in short, it is a regulation which will enable us to help put the european union on safer wheels.
member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, thank you for your contributions to this debate. reducing the climate change impact of the transport sector is a key objective of our energy and mobility policies. given the contribution of tyres’ role and assistance in the fuel consumption of cars, buses and trucks, it is important to ensure that users are provided with up-to-date and accurate information about tyre performance. the aim of the commission proposal is therefore to strengthen the information requirements on fuel efficiency, noise and safety aspects of tyres and make labels more visible and reliable. i encourage the european parliament to fully support the ambition of the commission proposal to ensure that tyre users can make the best choice from an energy efficiency and safety perspective, and can gain financially. i look forward to our further discussions on this important proposal in the next phases of the co—decision process.
madam president, the calamity has struck the central regions of mozambique following the passage of cyclone idai. the data points to a 90% destruction of the city of beira. nearly 800,000 people are affected and the confirmed death toll stands at 447. in addition to the devastation of the region, there is a risk of outbreaks of epidemics, including malaria, filariasis and cholera, and several deaths with symptoms of the latter have already been recorded. the united nations has appealed for emergency aid of two hundred and fifty million euros specifically for mozambique. the european union has announced a total of 3.5 million in aid not only to that country but also to zimbabwe and malawi, which are also affected, an amount which must be rapidly increased by providing, in addition to financial aid, material and food aid, life-saving equipment and medical assistance. other measures can and should be considered, such as channelling funds from the european development fund, or by triggering discussion on measures such as debt cancellation for these countries.

madam president, we must remember why this directive was created, it was not created because people have difficulty accessing culture or the internet, it was created because we have citizens who want to be able to enjoy the income of their work and cannot yet have everyone accessing and using this production without any kind of control. i would remind you that piracy and counterfeiting are crimes precisely because they subvert the laws of production and consumption, exploiting the labour of others and creating social inequality. in art, in culture, in journalism, it's exactly the same, and this is what we have to think about when we feel we have the right to use a text, a song or an image for our own benefit without paying the creator. if a creator wants to donate his work for free use he can do so, but if a creator wants to protect his right to use his work he can't, he doesn't have enough means today to protect it from the digital space. that is the purpose of this directive. it is a shame the manipulation of the citizens in this matter has prevented it from being given... mr president, i know that we are already in pre-campaign mode, but it is completely unacceptable to accuse cohesion policy of not having helped greece and my country, portugal. it's not just intellectual and political dishonesty, it's moral dishonesty. and so what we have done in this report is to ensure that cohesion policy continues, and will continue, to reduce inequalities and to contribute to a more social europe that is closer to its regions and its citizens. this is why special attention is paid to demographic, natural or structural handicaps which are permanent, such as the case of the outermost regions. it was only right that the guarantee for these regions from erdf and cohesion fund support should be included in the investments in waste facilities and management and environmental protection. another important measure was the maintenance of infrastructure funding at its airports. however, commissioner, i would like to say that i am very concerned about the effects of a thematic concentration at national level.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, every day in the european union, millions of citizens access digital content and services and sign contracts to do so. in several member states, however, there are no clear rules on the purchase of digital content and there are multiple and different rules on online and offline contracts. with this new package we are strengthening consumer rights, giving a common framework of rights to citizens and businesses and ensuring predictability for all, especially for small and medium-sized enterprises. we have thus removed the main contract law obstacles which hinder cross-border trade. including in-person sales was key. with only one set of rules for physical or virtual shops, we give legal certainty to businesses and consumers. i also welcome the fact that the acquired rights of consumers in some member states, such as portugal, have been safeguarded. full harmonization which does not respect the rights of these countries, which are more advanced in consumer protection, would be unfair and unacceptable. we want to improve consumer protection, not weaken it. the citizens' europe we are trying to build has the obligation to defend and guarantee these rights at all times.
mr president, the regulation we are considering ensures a sustainable energy transition in the sector of new passenger cars and new light commercial vehicles. it also stimulates innovation in new technologies that make the acquisition of zero- and/or low-emission vehicles affordable. it introduces real-world co2 emissions testing as a condition of vehicle type approval, which can help us to avoid fraudulent practices such as those identified by the parliamentary committee of inquiry into automobile emissions of which i was a member. i therefore welcome the rapporteur, mrs miriam dalli, and the whole team who have worked on this dossier for the extremely balanced proposal for a regulatory framework which makes it possible to articulate the transition to the mobility model, innovation and adaptation of european industry and safeguarding the interests of workers and taxpayers. we have to put this proposal for sustainable innovation into practice, we have to have the capacity to monitor, we have to have the capacity to train workers and develop territories sustainably to be one step ahead in modernity.

madam president, i apologize for this moment, mr vice-president, but we have had reports in this parliament before this proposal for a directive on the international governance of the oceans and the plastics strategy. in these two papers we understand both the importance of plastic and the problem that arises when these products do not have a proper end and end up floating in the ocean, with a negative impact on the biosphere, and i remind you that 80% of the ocean's waste originates on land. what we're going to need is to change our behavior and the way we relate to these products, to promote a more responsible, smarter consumption that can favor greener alternatives. i would point out that my proposal in the envi committee to include very lightweight plastic bags in the list of prohibited products was not adopted, but i would like to reiterate, ladies and gentlemen, that these bags are so light that they easily escape collection and are blown by the wind and end up in the ocean. the same is true for the unnecessary launching of balloons, and i would add that even though they are biodegradable and compostable materials, these characteristics do not apply in water. but to move from paper to action requires setting targets that allow member states to take action and measure its effectiveness. i therefore welcome all the ambitious targets that are included in the follow-up to this process.
madam president, i would like to welcome you and congratulate the rapporteur on his work in this parliament. it is never easy to draw up a budget for parliament, much less in an election year when amendments which should not even have been put to the vote, and which are even illegal, always appear, and therefore i would like to extend my warmest congratulations to the rapporteur, mr manka, and to all the shadow rapporteurs who have been and are involved in this project. the european people's party has always advocated rigor and transparency, but at the same time the necessary, sufficient means for the goal of legislative excellence. having bad legislation means a huge cost to the citizens of the european union. and we have done a great job in this parliament, a job which also requires quality services and also quality staff, and fortunately we have these high-quality services and excellent staff too. our aim of reducing costs as much as possible must not therefore conflict with principles such as multilingualism. it is necessary that each one continue to speak his own language in this space. there is also a need for translation. it is clear that savings can be made in this area, as we have done, but without reaching this principle, which is the principle of multilingualism. and then, of course, having a parlamentarium, having a house of europe or representations of the parliament in each member state, that has costs, but it is a way of helping to spread not only the work we do, but also the history of the european union and our values. we will continue to seek the balance between rigor, transparency and the necessary but minimal means for the goal of legislative excellence.
mr president, the drinking water directive has been in force for over 20 years and is one of the pillars of european union legislation in this area, providing quality supplies in over 99% of supplies. its review, as mentioned above, follows the first successful european citizens' initiative, right4water, which gathered 1.6 million signatures in favour of improving access to drinking water for all europeans. it is therefore a timely and necessary revision and provides for measures to facilitate access to water in public spaces and to align drinking water quality standards with the most up-to-date scientific parameters. i regret, however, that we have not gone further. some positive things can be highlighted: the reduction of concentration limits for certain pollutants such as lead, the introduction of new limits for endocrine disruptors, the start of the application of microplastics monitoring procedures, the promotion of the use of open sources and in this case the provision of tap water. and also the transition to a circular economy, helping to reduce the use of plastic bottles. we should have gone further, however, in particular in access to water and monitoring of microplastics. we fell short of the ambition of the citizens' petition, but it was a step forward on a path we must continue.
mr president, commissioner, this proposal to amend the directive on the quality of water intended for human consumption is of the utmost importance. it increases the direct costs of water supply, which are borne by the users, significantly, and it also increases the indirect public costs of monitoring and control, without this reflecting an increase in safety. it hinders the provision of public services, encourages privatisation and makes economic supply solutions, which can be safe and viable, particularly at local level, unfeasible. it goes beyond the recommendations of entities such as the world health organization, calls into question the functions of the state of supervision, supervision and control replaced by an alleged self-control of the concessionary companies of water services, is complacent with the non-compliant concessionaires. this proposal, in short, means more expensive, more unsafe, poorer quality water. only the european commission's unabashed submission to the interests of the transnational water companies can explain it.
mr president, i congratulate mr dantin on his excellent work on this report. i would like to recall the european citizens' initiative right2water, of which i was the shadow rapporteur, which reminded us that we have until 2030 to meet sustainable development goal 6 which concerns universal and equitable access to water. and the truth is that we are not only interested in debating the quality of the water we consume in europe, we are not only interested in that, but also in discussing the ways in which we can avoid the shortage of drinking water, aggravated by the effect of climate change, as well as access to water as a fundamental human right. this directive responds to the need to harmonise european minimum criteria which we are proud of, both in terms of quality and accessibility itself. in addition to these factors, this directive also brings greater transparency to the union water sector and the recognition that citizens, as consumers, have a right to be informed about the origin, quality and efficiency of the distribution systems, for which we all pay, directly or indirectly. ensuring the quality of water for human consumption in europe is not only a guarantee of our public health, but also one of the pillars of social justice.

information that was in france had not been transmitted. not often for lack of goodwill, but for lack of this system, this quick, expeditious remedy, which allows the authorities, having doubts, with security and respect for the law, protecting data, to do so. that can't happen again. the security of our citizens, the effective control of our borders, the fight against crime and terrorism must really be a priority and if they are not a priority our lack of response is unfortunately measured later in the number of victims and in the world of crime. and we are elected precisely to, to the mr president, these proposals extend not only the collection and use of biographical and biometric data - requiring harmonization of information systems - but also the ease with which the authorities can access this body of personal data without the need for judicial authorizations. this is not a purely technical option, but rather a fundamental political option which opens the door to the creation of a surveillance system at the level of the european union. proposals that infringe on the right to privacy and the freedom of citizens themselves deliberately fuel the confusion between migration, crime and terrorism. in practice, this means imposing the principle of the presumption of guilt: everyone is now treated as a potential criminal. today, they are third-country nationals, tomorrow they will be citizens of member states. the sovereignty of states is compromised by imposing a supranational framework which reinforces the unacceptable security drift and the xenophobic and racist response to migration. this is yet another example of how typical views of the far right find their way into the hands of the right and social democracy.
the rapporteur. - mr president, two final remarks: firstly, to the authorities, namely the police, who risk their lives every day to guarantee our freedoms. a second note of contradiction to what we have heard from the greens and the communists. the greens argue that with this system we can check criminals, but also people who are serious. i'd say that's normal, because if we knew who the criminals were, we wouldn't have to investigate anything. it is precisely because they are all mixed together that we must investigate everywhere so that among the honest people, who fortunately in this world are the majority, we can detect the criminals and thereby prevent, in particular, terrorist fighting and a normal flow from the external borders into the european union. as for the communists - who are so concerned about these questions of legality - i would also like to say, although they have been absent because it seems that the debate will not be important enough, that i can guarantee with absolute certainty that here in the european union we will always do everything with much more legality and respect for others and data protection than, for example, the regimes that the communists defend every day whether in venezuela, cuba or north korea, where, yes, there, in an intrusive manner, without respect for human rights, all information is surely collected, arbitrary detentions are made, even for the crime of opinion, and at the limit, people simply disappear at the hands of the state. that said, our concern as members of the european parliament is to ensure that the law is strictly observed and that the people are safe.
the european peace facility proposed by the commission, which this parliament is preparing to approve, is another example of how the european union masks its policies. the ideal of peace is sold abroad, an ideal used to justify the mobilization of substantial funds for defence and armaments. in practice, what this mechanism will finance is not only the european union's military missions but also the strengthening of the military and defence capabilities of third countries. we reject the path that the right wing and the social democrats are trying to impose by feeding the european union's warmongering, interventionist character. those who promote war cannot defend peace. the defence of peace, as the constitution of the portuguese republic advocates, requires the dissolution of nato or military structures in the context of the european union and the promotion of a policy of disarmament, including nuclear disarmament, and the resolution of conflicts mediated in full respect of international law and the charter of the united nations.
we are also very pleased that the commission has taken the initiative to introduce a new programme for the protection of the environment. this increase does not, however, overlook the lack of investment in cultural support and the deep precariousness of industrial relations in the sector, leaving artists in a situation of enormous dependence. this is a reality that is not separate from the austerity policies that the european union has imposed. but this proposal encourages a number of aspects which we are not following, it ties the possibility of support to a so-called european added value and subsequently to the policies of the european union and its vision for democracy or external relations. it is also sick of a mercantilist view of culture based on competition, which we reject. the example of the european youth orchestra and the reference to the need to diversify revenues and sources of funding is paradigmatic of this view. approaches that contribute to cultural normalisation and colonization that are contrary to the promotion of freedom and creative independence and the democratisation of culture.
mr president, i congratulate sirpa and mr bas on the excellent work they have done on this dossier. harmonising sustainable investment means bringing more transparency and competitiveness to investment within the union and not being subject to twenty-eight different systems which only contribute to market fragmentation and asymmetries in europe. fighting climate change is going to cost us money. the fact that we have succeeded in moving the economic cloud to water the green areas means that we have fully adopted the ecological guidelines on which europe is betting. if we are geared towards decarbonising the economy, we can only consider sustainable economic activities those that do not contribute to carbon dependence effects, nor produce non-renewable or environmentally harmful waste. we want to create an environment favourable to green investment and therefore information to investors has to be transparent in all businesses so that they can exercise their preferences with all the awareness and commitment that this entails and it is important that they know when they are investing in brown businesses so that they can be held accountable. we must have the courage to disagree when our conscience dictates otherwise.

that the sustainability of fisheries must combine the protection of species with the social protection of the operators in the sector, and that it is not acceptable for fishermen to bring home only 5 movement. i come from a country of immigrants, portugal, and i know how important it is that the citizen's card is recognised in other european countries. the fact is that even today many portuguese people face obstacles in accessing basic services in other member states because of the non-conforming format of our citizen's card. with this regulation we must make life easier for all europeans by removing unnecessary obstacles to the exercise of their rights. a portuguese citizen card will be valid and recognised anywhere in the european union. but we will also bring the visual aspect of identity cards closer together, in particular by including the european union flag next to the country reference. we are expressing the complementarity of european citizenship. last but not least, we've enhanced the security features. among many others, all cards will have the fingerprints of their holder. some, in the pre-election climate, will give in to the temptation to exploit this issue by inventing problems and fears. but we can't gamble with everyone's safety. by including fingerprints, we are taking an unprecedented step in the fight against identity theft, in the fight against madam president, i would like to stress at the outset that, after hearing what has been said here and the various parties, i will vote in favour of this proposal because i understand that it will not compete with national systems. it will open the door to people who have saved up money to have a new system, a complementary system, not competitive with national systems but complementary. this is not for everyone, nor does it call into question the national systems which can continue to operate under the universal compulsory model for everyone. this is for those people who now have some savings, some savings, and who invest it sometimes in bad investments and in bad products and who can now invest a little bit better in their future. i therefore think that this proposal is a good one and is precisely intended to complement and not compete with the national systems.
madam president, commissioner, if we really want a more social europe, then this europe must respond to the new demands of the new society. and what is this society? it's a society where families have their children later and later, where women are more and more, well, active in the workplace, where fathers, men, are also demanding their rights to fatherhood, and very well. what we found was that in our europe we had a great divergence between the rights of men and women, that in fact they are not in agreement with these family needs, that couples in fact have difficulty in accompanying their children, not only when they are born but also at school age when the needs of the children do not coincide with the work needs of companies. this has terrible consequences for the declining birth rate and the ageing of our europe, as we all know. on the other hand, this generation that has children later and later is also a sandwich generation because they are not only having to take care of their children, but also have to take care of their parents, and aging in europe brings us this increased responsibility. our elderly must be cared for and must be able to stay at home with greater stability. and their children, who are of working age, have no rights to work in the sense of being able to accompany their parents properly. that is why we urgently need to make an evolution in this family follow-up. unfortunately, we have not gone as far as i have proposed, but i am sure that this is indeed the challenge of the social europe of the future.
mr president, this proposal for a directive is an example of social regression and progress in labour exploitation. the definition of non-compulsory leave periods, the definition of pay on the basis of sickness benefits, paternity leave, the non-definition of pay on parental leave or the absence of pay on carers' leave are examples which would be enough to embarrass those who propose or advocate them. conditionalities are added to the needs of the employer and to factors such as working time or type of relationship before the leave application, in formulations that may exclude from access to leave, for example, temporary workers. this directive does not defend parents, it defends employers who will determine when, how and whether workers can enjoy their rights. we have put forward proposals that would raise social rights and labour protection from extending leave to payment of 100% of the reference wage, from protecting and promoting breastfeeding and nursing mothers to promoting public policies and services, proposals which were rejected by the european parliament, reasons which lead us to reject this proposal.
the president of the united states' declarations to recognize the golan heights as israeli territory - a unilateral declaration - and the declaration by the newly elected prime minister of israel, benjamin netanyahu, to annex territories in the west bank are two declarations and two intentions that call into question the middle east peace process. they not only call into question the middle east peace process, but they violate international law and the very agreements between the european union and the state of israel. in this connection, we think it is urgent to discuss and find out what the european union's position is and we therefore ask that mrs federica mogherini, in her capacity as vice-president and high representative, make a statement following the debate on libya and that we all discuss all these unacceptable intentions, both of the president of the united states and of the newly re-elected prime minister of israel.
