i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. every woman has the right to be protected from violence. the most dangerous place for a woman is her home, because that is where the most violent people are: as partners, as former partners, as family members. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the problem of the illegal trade in human beings. right now, the holidays are about to get especially dangerous. the european parliament has been asked to take a decision on the matter, and i am sure that the commission will be able to do so. it's about derailed control fantasies, wounded egos, that result from the fundamentally unequal distribution of power and resources in our society. we can do something about this by ratifying the istanbul convention. it is true of poland, it is true of austria, it is true of estonia, it is true of italy, it is true of all of europe.
mr. chairman, i would believe the istanbul convention to be exclusively about violence against women, children or any other violence, not about imposing some new social norms. indeed, if we want to fight violence against women, then we would not remove women and children from their homes and create additional stress for them, but we would remove the abuser. ladies and gentlemen, have you ever been to one of these safe houses? do you think they're really safe? do you know how many times the abuser broke into those houses, abused the women in front of the children, in front of other women? and the government agencies didn't want to report it because they didn't get the money, they wouldn't get the money for it. so let's get the abuser out of the house. let's get him to go to anger-control and then i'll believe something will happen. so, when we fight violence like this, then i will believe in the istanbul convention, and then abused women will report that violence much more. and this is just a play on words.
mr president, violence against women can take many forms, with dramatic expression in domestic violence, but also in prostitution or violence at work. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the protection of the euro against counterfeiting is a very important one. the fight against violence against women is therefore inseparable from the elimination of labour exploitation, inequalities and discrimination between men and women, and is inseparable from the commitment to fulfil women's rights, to create the conditions for them to live, work and participate on an equal basis. in addition to combating the economic and social causes of exploitation, inequalities and discrimination, which in themselves feed and reproduce the various dimensions of violence, it is essential to tackle cultural factors, by intervening to overcome prejudices and stereotypes, by stimulating the change of behaviour and mentality, by raising social awareness that equality in life is not only a just aspiration of women but also a condition for progress, social justice and democratization of peoples.
madam president, job insecurity is a plague that affects young and old alike and has serious repercussions on their working lives, but also on their future pensions. the causes of this precariousness are well known: low wages, irregular working hours, whether by shift work or by overloading working hours and mechanisms such as time banks; unstable work ties that do not protect workers in case of illness or unemployment, for example, research grants, internships, green vouchers, which deprive workers of social support; digital platforms that, under the guise of modernity, deprive their workers of their rights. precariousness has entered the vocabulary and the lives of workers and the unemployed and tends to be normalised. for example, the proportion of temporary workers in my country is higher than the european average. it's hard to get employment rights or get into a career, and even those in the career see obstacles to advancement. the european union is promoting this path. for us, precariousness and the reduction of labour rights are not an inevitability, they are an option which will always be opposed by us.
madam president, i take this opportunity to draw attention once again to the violent attacks which continue to be perpetrated against indigenous peoples by the ruthless extractive industries, often without a response in time, if not with the consent of the political powers. this is the case in brazil, colombia, bolivia, ecuador, venezuela and other countries in latin america, which has become the deadliest region for indigenous peoples and environmentalists. the recent death of paulo paulino of the guajajara tribe in brazil, or the attack on the nasa community in southwestern colombia in which five people were killed, are just recent examples illustrating an unacceptable reality. violence, intimidation, harassment and forced displacement of indigenous communities are increasingly common. the european union must be proactive in defending these communities. the united nations declaration on the rights of indigenous peoples and ilo convention 169 are clear in the rules and limits they set. we must use the instruments at our disposal to ensure that they are complied with.
madam president, to the presidency of the council and to first vice—president timmermans, i want to say that europe really needs institutions that work. tomorrow, we’ll face a vote for the new commission and the opportunity to really start meeting citizens’ expectations. it is time to deliver on decarbonisation, on the european pillar of social rights, and on a fair digital economy. the commission has indeed an ambitious agenda in which we recognise many of our proposals, but we won’t come even close to europeans’ hopes and dreams if we keep stalling everything that is relevant. in one word, we need to put our money where our mouth is. so let us demand from the council nothing less than an ambitious budget for the next term. it is time to stop demanding more and more from the european policies, while wanting to pay even less for it. we can do it. we can bridge the gaps by bringing own resources to the table. it is time to go back to basics. this union is about sharing a common dream of peace and sustainable development for all and, in this case, solidarity pays back through the reinforcement of the single market. therefore it benefits all europe, particularly those that tend to say that they just pay for the others. quo vadis, europe? where are the resources we need for the new priorities to come alive?
mr president, i would like to begin by joining those who have congratulated the rapporteurs on this 2020 budget. in fact, from what i was given to observe - i was a passive rather than an active agent - they did indeed do an excellent job in the final stage of the negotiations. and why do i say this? i say this because what has been achieved in this budget, compared with the commission's proposal, has indeed been significant progress and has been significant progress in what are the priorities of this parliament. i am going to start with the climate issue, with the issue of research and innovation, but also with the young people. the youth employment programme, the youth guarantee and the erasmus programme were used to provide answers to young people. that said, am i satisfied with the result? you can't be satisfied with the result. i think that this result is insufficient to finance the challenges of the european union in 2020, but it is the result that has been achieved, and so it is with this budget that we will be living in 2020. my concern also arises, as it must, from the fact that i am the rapporteur for the multiannual financial framework, and i would like to say the following: firstly, the figure of 0.99% is indeed a very low figure compared with the programme and the ambition of this parliament. what we need in the multiannual financial framework is an amount which is actually sufficient to finance the political priorities of the european union and above all the political priorities which we are going to vote on tomorrow when we elect the commission, because we are actually voting on a political programme. finally, i would like to thank commissioner oettinger for his commitment to transferring this way of getting the institutions to cooperate to his successor in the future commission.
madam president! the consequences of climate change are hurricanes, floods, droughts, avalanches, but most of all, human tragedies. every year there are fewer days of snow and more days of sunshine. this affects human health and has costs for the economy and agriculture. extreme weather events are destroying our homes and threatening our cultural heritage. increasingly frequent droughts are causing millions of people to migrate. we must be aware that existing systems such as copernicus and funds such as the european union solidarity fund allow us to warn of natural disasters or to remedy their effects, but they do not prevent them. without tackling climate change, without realizing that it's not just europe that has to pay the price, but the whole world, led by big corporations, is obliged to fight global warming and emissions, without thinking about the responsibility for future generations, we will be dealing with more and more, bearing more and more costs and mourning more and more victims.
madam president, as we know, disasters are deeply unjust: the european union is not a party to the gatt, but it is a party to the gatt. if there is one area where solidarity in the european union and the principle of cohesion should have a visible expression, it is in the protection of people, the economy and the environment in the face of disasters. the problem of disasters has been the subject of several resolutions in this parliament. follow-up is necessary because of the recommendations, by putting them into practice. this is the case with the creation of a financial framework appropriate to disaster prevention, which allows, among other measures, the correction of risk-inducing situations. unfortunately, very little has been done and the european commission bears particular responsibility for this delay. we believe that a community approach to disaster prevention should include, as a central concern, the reduction of disparities between regions and member states in this area, in particular by helping to improve prevention in regions and member states with high risk exposure and lower economic capacity.

(applause)
the joint text agreed by the conciliation committee on 18 november was approved by both parliament and the council. the budgetary procedure was implemented in accordance with article 314 of the treaty on the functioning of the european union. the budgetary procedure for the financial year 2020 can therefore be considered to be concluded. i therefore declare the budget of the european union for the financial year 2020 definitively adopted and shall now proceed to sign it. that concludes the voting.
president-in-office of the council. – madam president, john f. kennedy once said: ‘the ignorance of one voter in a democracy impairs the security of all’. i believe that maliciously-influenced or misled voters could pose a similar risk to our societies. evolving malicious interference and online manipulation shows that this risk to our democratic institutions is real and requires our constant attention. we are therefore grateful for this new opportunity to engage with you on this important topic. let me repeat that our presidency’s priority is to make sustained efforts to raise awareness, increase preparedness and strengthen the resilience of our democracies to disinformation. hybrid threats, including foreign interference in our democracies, remain, therefore, at the top of our political agenda. the presidency has taken a number of steps, in particular a dedicated and permanent horizontal working party of the council was created during the first month of our presidency. it discusses how to strengthen the eu’s capabilities in countering hybrid threats and to build resilience in the eu and member states. the mandate of the group includes election interference and disinformation campaigns. we have proposed council conclusions to be adopted in december on complementary efforts to enhance resilience and counter hybrid threats, building on the important work done at the eu level in recent years. the conclusions also pay attention to countering disinformation and securing free and fair elections. the last debate held in this house in september sought broad agreement that foreign interference and disinformation pose a major threat to democracy and eu values. without a doubt, the eu institutions and the member states have an important role to play; this is in the remit of their competencies in the protection of democratic processes. it is therefore appropriate that protecting european democracy is also a priority for president von der leyen. we have in particular noted with great interest that vice-president jourová will be responsible for coordinating the work on a europe and democracy action plan that will include a set of initiatives. this will require a focus on building up resilience and countering disinformation while pursuing and protecting fundamental freedoms. countering hybrid threats requires a comprehensive approach and a horizontal working method. this is why the work needs to be done in close alignment with all relevant stakeholders in the eu institutions as well as in the member states to enhance the eu’s ability to prevent, detect and respond to hybrid threats. protection of our democracies and elections involves concerted efforts by the eu and the member states, but also by civil society and industry. our response has to be comprehensive, with a focus on both the internal and external dimensions of the threat. much has been done already, both by the eu institutions and individual member states. let me just briefly mention the main milestones, such as regular exchanges in the european election cooperation network, setting up the rapid alert system, enhancement of strategic communication, facilitating networks of independent fact checkers, promotion of media and digital literacy and awareness-raising activities, and monitoring of the implementation of the code of practice by social media platforms. but it is also important that we further develop institutional mechanisms and tools to fulfil the objectives set by the european council. it is also essential to continue developing partnerships, especially our cooperation with nato. in this regard, the helsinki european centre of excellence for countering hybrid threats also contributes to fostering cooperation in this field. likewise, disinformation strategies by foreign actors are deployed beyond our borders. so we need to work with partners in the eastern and southern neighbourhood and in the western balkans. as stressed in the conclusions on democracy adopted by the council last month, exposing disinformation, effective scrutiny over the financing of political messaging, strengthening media, information and digital literacy skills are key elements in this regard. we need to act together in a structured and coordinated way in order to tackle these challenges. protecting elections is at the core of defending our european values.
member of the commission. – madam president, thank you for giving me the opportunity to take the floor on the issue of interference from other countries in our democracies and elections. the commission has been deeply engaged in broad efforts to address the manifold risks stemming from lack of transparency, disinformation and other manipulations of electoral and democratic processes, and is committed to strengthening this path in the upcoming term. indeed, as we heard from the presidency, it will be one of my biggest tasks in the new commission. thank you for the support. the conduct of free and fair elections is primarily the responsibility of member states. nevertheless, a european approach is necessary given the cross-border dimension of efforts to manipulate democracy and the importance of joining efforts to address the threats. just as with the rule of law, what affects one member state can affect all and impact the union itself. the threats at stake are complex and the targets are constantly evolving. for instance, external actors are more often using domestic proxies. we also see new technological developments allowing for new forms and strategies of interference. in the context of the 2019 elections, the commission has taken a number of concrete steps to address the challenges, including with the election package of measures to secure free and fair elections in europe, with the code of practice on disinformation, which has been signed by facebook, twitter, google and microsoft, and with the action plan on disinformation, with which we have also made some progress. we will build on this work in the upcoming term. we have worked to put a holistic and permanent framework in place, with an aim to strengthen the resilience of our democracies and adapt to the evolving threats and coordinated and intentional manipulation. to address the threats at stake, a coordinated and comprehensive approach is necessary, involving all actors across government, including regulatory and enforcement authorities, political parties, journalists, fact-checkers, researchers, educators and society and civil society at large, as well as industry and the online platforms. some of them i mentioned, but of course we need to invite more to work with us. and of course we will need citizens to work on strengthening their own resilience against these new threats. i sometimes say that we have to work on increasing the demand for truth, and this will be a very big task ahead of us. we need to get better when it comes to detecting, analysing and exposing disinformation and other manipulations of european democratic processes, whatever the tactics and actors employed. this needs to go hand in hand with wider efforts on monitoring cybersecurity and resilience against hybrid threats. in terms of cooperation, we can be pleased with the european cooperation network on elections, which meets today for the fifth time to discuss its future work on safeguarding elections. this is a new club and a very important piece of our toolkit. we also need to further enhance cooperation between eu institutions and eu member states via this network and the rapid alert system, which will help us build a genuine community of practitioners. it will also allow us to build meaningful cooperation with international partners, such as the g7 or nato, because we are facing a real global problem. we can improve the effectiveness of communication through a coordinated response to disinformation incidents, as well as raising public awareness more generally. to this end, it is important to pursue our efforts aimed at facilitating the establishment of an independent community of fact-checkers and academic researchers to detect and expose disinformation campaigns across social networks. following its initial 12-month period of implementation, the code of practice on disinformation is now in the process of being evaluated. should the results under the code prove unsatisfactory, the commission may propose further measures, including of a regulatory nature. we also need to invest in strengthening media literacy, which is key to resilient and democratic societies. citizens must be empowered to have the adequate tools for a well-informed participation in democratic processes. president-elect von der leyen has proposed a new digital services act, which will upgrade our liability and safety rules for digital platforms, services and products. she has also pledged to put forward a coordinated european approach on artificial intelligence. these efforts will be vital if we are to ensure a prosperous, safe and democratic digital future for europe. building on the political guidelines of president-elect von der leyen, i will in the new commission lead on the work towards a european democracy action plan addressing the threat of interference in our european elections, whilst being mindful of the need to protect the freedom of expression. i will look into possible legislative proposals to ensure clearer rules on the financing of european political parties and greater transparency on paid political advertising. in our actions we must never lose the balance. our goal is to protect our european democracy. so it is clear that the respect for freedom of speech and our fundamental rights and values must be our cornerstones.

mr. president, this is not the first time we've talked about fake news and foreign interference. we were standing here a year ago. two months ago, we had this debate. we adopted a resolution last month and we are here again today. we're back here because it's still important and because it seems like there's no real action. the european elections are over. we've seen several studies that show that almost half of the 250 million europeans were exposed to fake news from russian trolls in the run-up to those elections. we've seen networks operating in different countries to spread disinformation. users and pages have been removed from facebook and other social networks, but only after they have been viewed hundreds of millions of times. fact-checkers from our own east stratcom task force recorded over 1,000 cases of russian interference, more than doubling from the previous year: the commission has also confirmed that it is a clear confirmation of the challenge facing us all. the task force has been expanded, but is that enough? i asked the eu's foreign service that question and the answer was very clear: the budget does not allow us to tackle the problem adequately. so we have to do more. less talk, more action. i would like to call on the commission to really tackle this, certainly in the coming period. this must be done in two specific areas. resources, more resources to really address the problem. more money for analysis and research, more money for anti-disinformation campaigns, more investment in technology, more commitment to our own resilience and our awareness of all of us. second, the tech giants: end the "freedom, happiness" approach to platforms like facebook. it is high time they finally took responsibility. there are no voluntary codes of conduct, but binding measures to enforce transparency and cooperation.
madam president, mr lenaers just said that we have just had a resolution, we’ve just had a debate and now, of course, we’re back debating the same thing, but there is a reason for this and the reason is that something is happening. this is not an exaggerated issue; this is an issue on which the commission – commissioner jourová and commissioner king – have been working now for months. this is an issue on which we have been working. we wanted a special committee for a reason and the reason is not that some donald trump says to boris johnson, i like you, i want your nhs, i’d like to buy it. that is not the interference we’re talking about. i don’t like it, but it is not what we’re talking about. it is talking about a growing body of evidence that there is illegal and covert interference, what john f kennedy would not have liked. because at least one voter is voting differently because they have been targeted. cambridge analytica, the theft of private information for covert and illegal use against a constitution and against a body of jurisprudence and law. so let’s just be clear what we are talking about. it is illegal and it is covert. today it will harm us; maybe tomorrow it will harm you. so, this is what we are talking about and i welcome what my uk colleagues have done in initiating this debate because what they are doing is uncovering the latest element of this in my own country, which is where our conservative government is covering up the truth from the public by refusing to release the intelligence and security committee (isc) report on the russian threat to uk politics. one of our big member states of the european union. the report has been approved by the uk intelligence agencies, there is no impediment to publication, and yet, they’re not publishing. how much more evidence do we need to understand that this evidence is real? madam president, the defence of democracy requires a pluralistic, democratic and independent media sector. it calls for the defence of the independence of the media from economic power, preventing the monopolistic concentration of media companies and advocating the strengthening of the public service, guaranteeing the rights of journalists and other information professionals and their respect for ethical and deontological principles. the increasing concentration in the european union, which serves the interests of the dominant political and economic groups, is moving in the opposite direction, limiting the freedom and independence of the press, facilitating the dissemination of lies or the silencing of communist and progressive forces, their proposals and actions, while promoting imperialism, obscurantism, populism and their anti-democratic aims, opening the door to the far right. the so-called external threats that the european union and this parliament insist on pointing out are only a pretext to mask this reality and to continue on the dangerous path of single and reactionary thinking.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the digitalisation of our societies and economies creates opportunities for everyone, citizens and businesses, new products and better services, solutions to the second, but also opens the door to more sophisticated forms of evading the law and taxation. we must anticipate the problems of the future and decide in the present with the speed and intelligence that this age of innovation demands. vat is an important source of tax revenue for all member states and for the union, which has a source of revenue of its own. harmonizing rules and approximating tax policies in the vat area is to protect the integrity of the internal market and the free movement of goods and services based on fair tax rules. combating tax fraud and evasion is a priority to strengthen citizens' trust in democratic institutions and public administrations across europe. vat is based on communication by operators in a given value chain and is therefore based on trust. while it is true that the overwhelming majority of these agents are reliable and fulfil their obligations, vat fraud unfortunately remains a problem. the difference between the estimated vat and the vat actually collected in 2017 was eur 137,5 billion. and this means a loss of 267 euros for every european citizen. vat fraud in e-commerce in the union amounts to eur 5 billion annually, the equivalent of the product of all e-commerce in the portuguese market in one year. the reports we are debating today are the expression of the will of this parliament to go further in the fight against this fraud which harms us all. at the same time, we have created a central european system for the collection and exchange of payment data and strengthened the obligations of payment services in collecting, retaining and reporting such data. our amendments are intended to signal the will to be more ambitious and to confirm parliament's commitment to a more effective fight against vat fraud. let me therefore highlight a few key aspects: the increase in the retention period ensures that the authorities have more time to identify possible fraud situations. the setting of a minimum value, which activates the obligation to report by the payment service in a single transaction, reduces the space to circumvent the system we are approving. with express reference to the obligations towards the european data protection supervisor, we would point out that no action by public authorities or payment services infringes the fundamental right of europeans to privacy and the protection of their personal data. we support the use of new technologies in payment systems, call for modernisation and stress the need to simplify procedures for businesses and citizens. ladies and gentlemen, what we're talking about here today is really about fairness, about making sure that everyone, regardless of country or their digital skills, pays their fair share. financial technology is changing, every day, the way we consume, trade and pay for the services we hire. and we need to adapt our legislation to the new realities of the digital world. vat fraud knows no borders and can only be tackled by increased cooperation between national authorities and european institutions. it is up to us to ensure greater equity, to ensure fairer and more efficient taxation, to level the playing field in a struggle, which has been uneven up to now, between those who seek to evade their obligations and those who defend tax justice.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. these reports reflect a deliberate ambition, based on the certainty that digitalisation is changing our lives every day. legislation today therefore means more than finding general and abstract solutions: it also means finding concrete solutions to problems which, in detail, can become significant losses, losses to the public purse of the member states, to the general budget of the union and, indeed, to the family budget of europeans. vat fraud in e-commerce in the european union is increasing because e-commerce in the union is increasing. often, protecting the internal market requires that we know how to wait and let the market work. that's not the case. we are legislating in this way because we believe in a fair market in which taxation and tax obligations can be an instrument of justice and protection for businesses and citizens. it is true that these reports are about vat in electronic commerce, but they can be a signal from this sector for the great vat reform we want for europe. a reform for a taxation more adapted to the challenges of the digital economy, more ambitious in building a european budget focused on cohesion, convergence and competitiveness. taxes are inevitable, as we know. our challenge is to make them a tool for our economies, growth and jobs.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group, madam president, during the mario draghi years extraordinary monetary policy instruments were mobilised. however, growth in these years has been mediocre and these instruments have risks and consequences that should not be ignored. in the hearings with the members who will now be appointed, we wanted to know if they agree with the importance of having a policy mix in the european union, namely with much more significant budgetary instruments than those that currently exist, we wanted to know what they think about the ecb looking more at the issue of employment and inequalities, even if that means more latitude in the targets for inflation, and we wanted to know what ideas they have for the ecb to be able to have a role in responding to climate change or at least not mess up. the answers we have received from fabio panetta have been, from our point of view, satisfactory, which is why we will support his candidacy. mrs schnabel's answers were not as satisfactory, but we also do not want to make it impossible precisely because of concerns about gender balance. we join all the groups who have spoken about the importance of member states taking their responsibilities in this objective which is an objective that should unite us all to have gender balance in future appointments.
madam president, a new political cycle is now well under way. a bold agenda for the future of europe was set and it is now time to deliver. we expect the european central bank – its executive board, from its president to those who are now appointed – to play their role in the european priorities. we have to move forward, being able to agree on smaller things, but also on the hard reforms. we cannot keep betting on the prolongation of this economic cycle without doing anything else. we cannot rely solely on monetary policy, which is of the utmost importance but is not sufficient. we need a strong investment plan and we have to build a budget for the stabilisation that we know we’ll need in the difficult moments that eventually will come sometime. we need to act on the climate and on the economy, but also on social cohesion. the ecb has an important role and we have to analyse its mandate, taking into account modern european priorities. but we will also hold these executive board members accountable for its president’s pledge to keep the previous stance of monetary policy throughout this next term because this is where it will make a difference: if we have from the european central bank and from its executive board the trust that monetary policy will keep its adaptive stance. and then we have to do the rest on the rest of the economic policy.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group, madam president, we see this text as an effort to combat the race to the bottom in the taxation of large multinational companies. this race to the bottom has resulted in a huge loss of tax revenue which is then borne in part by the other taxpayers, particularly those who live by their work, but also by the degradation of public services and public investment policies in the member states. but it is also a violation of the principle of a fair relationship between member states, because some member states design strategies to expropriate other member states of the tax revenue that is due to them. and finally, it is here and now, in an institution where there is so much discussion of undistorted competition, the level playing field, a violation of the principle of undistorted competition, not only between companies from different tax jurisdictions, but also between companies that have the ability to evade taxes using these instruments and companies that do not have that ability. it is a disappointment on our part that it has not been made clear in this text that when we discuss minimum taxation at international level, we must obviously talk about effective taxation, because otherwise we will simply be continuing the game of tax evasion with new rules. and one thing, at least, we can say that it has become clear, that the proponents of this resolution argue that the european union must take the initiative at this level and therefore will not resort to the usual excuses that yes, it is a fair fight, but it will only be possible when all the offshore countries in the world are in agreement.
madam president, commissioner gentiloni, with the impasse in the council, the path being taken in the oecd is an opportunity to respond globally to the challenges of taxation, particularly in the digital area. as regards the distribution of taxation rights, what is agreed will change the taxation principles in the global economy. the debate is moving in the right direction, albeit slowly. as regards the overall minimum taxation, the proposal for a minimum tax rate at the level of each jurisdiction, in particular for digital, is welcomed. in the meantime, the council is expected to move forward with proposals on company taxable income. but the commission and the member states must ensure that the negotiations on these two pillars are conducted jointly. finally, we hope that an agreement will be reached during the next year, at least during the german presidency of the council. this agreement will be key to ensuring greater tax equity and social justice in europe and the world.
mr president, representatives of the steel industry have warned the european commission several times this year that their sector is in crisis. the european commission replied that it had taken all measures to reverse this development. but the reality is different. companies are cutting production, laying off employees, and considering whether to continue their activities at all. arcelor mittal decided to suspend production in krakow, which led to the loss of 1,300 jobs. tata steel announced that it would cut 3,000 jobs in europe and others are closing their furnaces, including u.s. steel košice, where 2,500 people will lose their jobs. and that's not all. the commission therefore concluded that the union industry did not suffer material injury within the meaning of article 3 (5) of the basic regulation. although the european union pretends to take steps to be greener, it is liquidating its modern and environmentally friendly operations by increasing the quotas for dirty steel from third countries. the european commission says that it has responded well to the introduction of restrictive measures, but this is not true. and i ask you: what will you, as the new commission, do to help the steel industry in europe?

mr president, the european disability strategy 2010-2020 has proved to be an instrument to help improve the lives of millions of europeans with a wide range of disabilities. we have to acknowledge these advances, but we also have to acknowledge that there are still many problems to overcome, old problems, such as access to the labour market, and new problems, such as transition to adulthood, the aspiration to independent living and ageing. these are problems that require an effective, humanized and innovative response. that is why i think the commission's commitment to the definition of a new european strategy is so important and i would like to hear a greater commitment from the commissioner in this area. we must take a step forward towards a society which fully integrates these citizens in a context of full equality and freedom. and we need to do that with the involvement of the people and the organizations that represent them. people with disabilities must have a central voice in this process.
mr president, concrete measures are needed to defend the rights of disabled people and to give them the right to live independently. for this to happen, mobility, accessibility, information and inclusion need to move beyond a list of good intentions to become a reality. we believe that access to work with rights is fundamental to overcoming inequalities and to the integration of people with disabilities. states should promote measures to encourage their integration into the labour market, in particular through public policies on training and employment, by adapting labour legislation and the regulation of working hours to their needs and specificities, paying particular attention to the protection of pregnancy and maternity in the case of women with disabilities. even in a situation of dependency, people must have rights, and these rights also go beyond the rights of carers, where they exist, and the existence of a humanized and accessible public network that provides support to informal carers and people in care. this is what we stand for.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. i come from a country where we have a lot to do with bees because we grow a lot of fruit, and fruit, of course, needs pollinators, but it also needs protection. we live in this dilemma and have some experience of it. you have to look at this a little bit more closely. it is not just a question of what pesticides are used, but also how they are used. it is simply that during the flowering period pesticides have no place on fruit trees, they simply have to be banned. it's about working closely with the beekeepers, and it's about -- and we haven't talked much about this -- that the biggest problem for bees right now is not pesticides, and it's not biodiversity loss, but it's parasites that are attacking the bees themselves, so varroa, beetles and things like that, and unfortunately we're getting more and more of these parasites, and we need to work in research and development here as well, so that we can develop effective countermeasures.
member of the commission. – mr president, now, it’s abundantly clear that we all agree that pollinators are essential and that we need to protect them, and this is clear from everybody’s comments. the commission has recognised the urgency and promptly answered the calls of the european parliament and of the public to tackle the decline of pollinators, and we adopted the european pollinators initiative, which is an ambitious framework that can address this complex challenge. it does consolidate actions across european union policies and strengthen links between them, and to do this, we consulted broadly experts and stakeholders and introduced new actions where gaps were identified. now, in the light of the european green deal, actions addressing the main threats to pollinators do need to be scaled up and made more effective. to measure the effects of policies and measures, however, we also need a robust monitoring system for pollinators and the threats they face. i listened carefully to a lot of the questions. i agree it’s crucial to look at imports, and the upcoming evaluation of the pesticides legislation will look at the issue of import tolerances and open this discussion. the green deal (i heard this being mentioned) does in fact speak about reducing pesticides. it says we will reduce the risk and use of pesticides significantly, and the farm-to-fork strategy, which, as you all know, will be presented, hopefully, in the spring, will be more specific about specific targets. additionally, the commission has taken decisive action to ban and restrict the use of neonicotinoids. two are no longer approved, one cannot be used outdoors, and the commission will be proposing not to renew the approval of a fourth one. and it is supporting the use of low-risk and non-chemical pesticides. now, concerning – i think there was a question on emergency authorisations – we have increased scrutiny of these, including proposing decisions preventing two countries from granting contested authorisations. furthermore, the commission will continue supporting member states to ensure the sustainable use of pesticides, and the commission will be presenting the findings of the refit evaluation of the pesticides regulation and the second reports on the implementation of the sustainable use directive early in 2020. and finally, i agree that having an ambitious strategy plan on the cap is very important in order to deliver on our own ambitions. now let me be absolutely clear: the commission is determined to coordinate and steer a decisive european union action to stop and reverse pollinator decline. however, the success of this initiative will, critically, depend on the strong engagement of all stakeholders in its implementation. member states will have an indispensable role in the deployment of effective actions and monitoring systems on the ground, and we look forward to the european parliament’s strong endorsement of this initiative and the crucial role of member states in its implementation.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, honourable members, echoing what you and the commission have said, let me also conclude by recalling the importance of pollinators for biodiversity, for our food systems and for our well-being. i welcome this discussion today on this very important topic. it is clear that the current efforts on pollinators’ protection are not sufficient. we need to have a more holistic and coordinated approach on this issue, and there is broad support in the council to increase the environmental ambition in the cap – the common agricultural policy – reform, of course taking into account the situation of our farmers. moreover, the relevant eu measures have to be stronger and properly implemented. i am, therefore, looking forward to the new commission strategy, which will, hopefully, address the weaknesses of the current eu policies and will be accompanied by appropriate monitoring and enforcement mechanisms to improve their effectiveness. thank you very much for this important discussion.
i have received a motion for a resolution pursuant to rule 132 (2). the debate is closed. the vote will take place on wednesday 18 december 2019. the commission shall be assisted by the committee of the regions.
in writing. the survival of europe's bee population is under increasing threat from year to year, as is the number of wild pollinating species threatened with extinction. the main causes of this worrying trend based on the consistent conclusions of related scientific research are to be found in human activities. the eu pollinator initiative was an important milestone in the process of identifying the problem. however, the measures have so far proved insufficient to protect pollinating species. more ambitious action is therefore needed for three reasons. on the one hand, we have a moral responsibility to pass on to future generations the environment we inherited from our ancestors, at least in the same quality. on the other hand, 75% of european food production depends on pollination by bees, so strengthening the european beekeeping sector is of paramount importance. finally, without further action, the quality products of european beekeepers will not be able to compete with cheap imports, which could further reduce the self-sufficiency of the european union. the european parliament's resolution as a whole is trying to address these factors, and i support it.
the agenda includes the discussion of the council and commission statements on animal welfare conditions during transport to third countries (2019/2944 ((rsp)). i would remind you, ladies and gentlemen, that you can apply for the 'catch the eye' procedure and the blue card by using both the standard registration and the electronic system directly from your post. the instructions are available at the entrance to the chamber.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, we have all been struck by the news a few weeks ago about the capsizing of a vessel which led to the drowning of more than 14 000 sheep. i’m sure that we all agree that everything must be done to avoid a similar tragic accident happening again. animal welfare remains a serious concern for european citizens; 82% of respondents to a eurobarometer survey on this issue said that improvements are badly needed in farm-animal welfare, and most of them considered that the eu should do more to promote greater awareness of animal welfare, including at international level. the council regularly discusses matters related to animal welfare. over the last six months, the finnish presidency has worked very actively on the preparation of council conclusions on animal welfare. and ladies and gentlemen, i am pleased to confirm that the conclusions were adopted by the council yesterday. in these conclusions, the council emphasises the importance of good animal welfare as an integral part of sustainable animal production, and notes the improvements already achieved in the eu. at the same time, the council acknowledges the need to continue improving the welfare of animals, including during transport over long distances. on the issue of transport, as a first step, the council encourages the commission and the member states to continue their work on the effective enforcement of the existing legislation, in particular through improved exchange of information between the competent authorities of the member states, and more active cooperation between different competent authorities in member states and third countries. as a further step, the council invites the commission to review and update the existing transport legislation to take into account current practical problems and experience of member states in its enforcement, as well as more recent scientific knowledge and technical developments. dear friends, we are all aware that various sensitive issues such as the transport time or the sustainability of trade in live animals may need to be discussed in this context. these are very important questions which certainly deserve thorough consideration in different fora with the contribution of all stakeholders involved. finally, regarding international aspects, the council reaffirms the importance of promoting the welfare of animals globally, given the competition that eu farmers are facing in global trade. discussions with our trade partners, and in particular negotiations on free-trade agreements with third countries, offer indeed an excellent opportunity for this. naturally, this goes hand in hand with our continued efforts at multilateral level to develop standards, guidelines and other activities aimed at achieving good animal welfare practices worldwide, including during long-distance transport, for instance through the world organisation for animal health. thank you very much, mr president, for your attention and for this very timely debate.
member of the commission. – mr president, like you, i was terribly shocked by the news on 24 november that almost 15 000 sheep had drowned when the ship transporting them capsized in the black sea port of midia. it is, after all, our responsibility to ensure that animals are safe and protected during transport. animal welfare must remain an overriding consideration in our work. the information provided by the romanian veterinary authorities who approved the vessel for livestock transport and supervised the dispatch of the animals, was that available space requirements set out in the eu law were fully respected; but, of course, this is of little comfort as we reflect on the losses sustained on that day. the transporters clearly failed in their primary responsibility to protect the animals in their charge throughout the journey. this is a requirement specifically set out in eu legislation and which must be respected. they failed to take adequate precautions to prevent this loss and to protect the animals from the pain and the distress that they suffered before they perished. this is about the ethical values of our union. the commission is working with member states to improve the conditions of animals during transport. in recent years, we have taken action to improve the situation, particularly at the border between bulgaria and turkey, a well-known hotspot for animal welfare issues. since 2017, we have dedicated staff and resources to tackle this particular problem. although more can, and must, be done, the results are encouraging. by working closely with veterinary authorities in exporting members states and turkey, and with the cooperation of ngos, we have dramatically reduced the number of consignments that cross the borders during hot periods. last summer there wasn’t a single incident reported at the border linked to the road transport of animals. this does not mean that all issues have been resolved. it is nonetheless a signal that we are at least on the right track. we need to maintain a constant dialogue with member states and stakeholders through the network of animal welfare contact points and the animal welfare platform, and we need to ensure that this dialogue leads to concrete results and actions. actions on sea transport have also been taken. in the last two years we audited official control arrangements for sea transport of livestock in four member states, including romania. for these audits, we saw that the situation is quite worrying. many arrangements seem to fall well below the standards established by eu law. our auditors found particular shortcomings concerning the approval of vessels by the authorities. i am well aware that the supervision and approval of vessels for animal transport are indeed challenging tasks. specific and extensive training is needed to adequately perform them – specialised training that differs from that provided to most veterinarians. the recent incident makes it abundantly clear that even ensuring that facilities are suitable to accommodate animals is no guarantee that the vessel itself is seaworthy, or that its crew is properly trained. there are also currently no arrangements to inform veterinarian authorities of problems detected by inspectors in other eu countries, and this is something that we are actively working on with the european maritime safety agency to allow for better sharing of information regarding livestock vessels. we have also worked with member states to improve their checks on livestock vessels, and this process has brought out some improvement in the inspection systems for these vessels. a further initiative that we have taken is to engage with the countries receiving live animals in order to get information on each consignment. this will provide valuable feedback on the conditions of the animals on arrival, and promote continuous improvement, and of course, as is usual, we have issued recommendations following our audits. we will closely monitor how these are addressed by member states. this is a matter that i will follow closely and i will raise with ministers on every occasion. they all need to take action to prevent incidents like the one that happened in romania from happening again, and i can assure you that i will work with the same focus to improve the conditions of animals transported by sea, as we did to improve the conditions of animals transported by road under the previous commission. i can also assure you that i will look at animal welfare as an important part of the farm to fork strategy that i will be presenting in spring 2020 as part of the european green deal, and this includes a critical look at the existing rules, including animal transport.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission is not the demise of the queen hind and the loss of thousands of animals is beyond regret. but i also expect that we will hear from the romanian authorities what really happened. why did this ship sink? was this ship capable of transporting so many animals? was it overloaded? have any current animal welfare rules been broken? it is no good continuing to call for new rules on animal welfare and the transport of animals, but on the other hand - and i am quite right in saying so, commissioner - we still have situations where the existing law is not sufficiently implemented. i think we have to ask the member states to ensure that the law that exists today, which is very strong in europe, is also implemented. the transport of live animals cannot always be prevented. we have to transport farm animals, for example, and there are animals that go to the final mast and they have to be transported alive. but there are also situations in europe where animals are dragged halfway across europe in search of the even cheaper slaughterhouse, and then the carcasses are transported back. i think we should take a look at this nonsense. i think we should transport the carcass wherever possible and not live animals. we should also do this where special slaughters, in this case, culling, are required by the customers. we must also contact the countries which then buy these animals so that we can supply them not with live animals but with slaughtered carcasses.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the animal health and welfare standards in the community are not exceeded. it is clear that the efforts of this parliament to achieve ambitious goals, which we must be proud of and which we must defend, are often frustrated. there is a need, commissioner, for a careful analysis of what action is needed to ensure that our animal welfare standards are fully and uniformly applied in all member states. as far as the committee on agriculture is concerned, we have already decided to go down this road, partly to reduce the distortive effects of the possibility that producers within the union can turn non-application of the rules into competitive advantages. our farmers, in complying with european standards, incur much higher production costs than their competitors outside the union, and thus find themselves victims of unfair competition. we can no longer accept that this should happen within the single market.
on behalf of the renew group. mr president, i hope that this serious accident in romania will make us aware of the importance of transport conditions for the treatment and welfare of animals. unnecessary stress and hazards are things that can be remedied by legislation and a general change of attitude, if there is a will to do so. there are ways, from education to legislation, and the commitment of the member states is paramount. mismanagement of animals, such as long transport journeys, crowding and heat, can be avoided if animals are in the hands of responsible operators. transport to third countries must be subject to strict surveillance. the starting point must always be that the transport of a live animal is a last resort. of course, sick and injured animals are a special case, and transportation is one of the factors that determines animal welfare. quality is more important than quantity, for even two hours of poor transport can be more severe than twice as much transport under good conditions. therefore, the density of the slaughterhouse network must also be considered, especially in countries with long distances. the welfare of animals must be the main objective of transport, and we must promote it more strongly, and therefore the same standards must be applied from the slaughterhouse, regardless of the country of origin. we have every opportunity in the eu to make responsible transport a strong competitive force on the world food market. in the legislative field, we must pay attention to two things. firstly, the rules already in place for the transport of animals must be fully implemented in each member state. secondly, the updating of the animal transport directive must also be strongly integrated into the farm to fork strategy and the green deal.
on behalf of the green group in the european parliament mr president, we are gathered here to witness the failure of european policies to protect animal welfare. on 24 november, more than 14 000 sheep drowned in the black sea after a live animal transport ship sank off romania, and this tragedy was expected as a 2018 report issued by the same parliament already denounced the serious shortcomings of european legislation and the systematic non-compliance of several member states with community regulations. moreover, reports from various non-governmental organisations show that there are no animal welfare conditions on board these vessels, nor are there any phytosanitary guarantees, which are often lacking in the countries receiving these animals, mainly in the middle east. in short, the trade in the brutal and inhumane exploitation of these animals can only be considered as an attack on any so-called 'developed' society, and there is therefore no other way but to end the export of live animals outside the european union. and the question i ask the new commission is whether it has the courage to put an end to this barbarity now.

vice-president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. – madam president, this exchange of views has been very informative and i thank the house for the opportunity to know more about your views on this difficult issue, namely relations with iran. i can assure you that we will not ignore the plight of the thousands of individuals in iran who were involved in, or affected by, the protests. i confirm our commitment to address these issues with iran, publicly and privately, until we’ve got clarity on the full scale of what has occurred and with the expectation that those identified as responsible for the violence will be held accountable. this is in general terms. i will try to answer some of the specific issues that you have raised. it has been asserted that support for the joint comprehensive plan of action (jcpoa) has prevented the european union from focusing on other issues. that’s not true. we have engaged in a frank and active dialogue with iran on all issues, maintaining a range of eu autonomous restrictive measures, including on human rights violations. we don’t believe that we would be in a better situation to address those without the jcpoa being in place. i can assure you that this is my strong belief. the jcpoa was first enshrined by a united nations security council resolution and was not an invention by europeans. it was the united nations security council which adopted this agreement and we view it as a crucial issue for regional and european security. no one wants to see a nuclear arms race in the region and no one has been able to come up with a peaceful way of ensuring that iran does not develop a nuclear weapon. having said this, we have on several occasions since june 2019 expressed our strong concern about iran’s reduction in its commitment and we will continue defending the objective and full obligation of this agreement. we will do our best in order to save the jcpoa agreement. other people say that we are too soft on iran. i want to engage with you in a frank and deep debate, and not to say that everybody is happy. no. let’s go to the real issues. are we too soft on iran? i don’t think so. the eu still has a number of sanctions in place: first, nuclear proliferation sanctions under the cpoa; second, human rights sanctions – currently 83 individuals and entities, including a ban on exports to iran of equipment which might be used for internal repression and equipment to monitor telecommunications because, when you disturb the traffic on the internet, you do that with sophisticated telecom instruments – and, third, sanctions under our syria and cp931 terrorist regime. so we are not being especially soft or hard. we do what we have to do and personally i think that you will understand that the jcpoa is something that is important for us, but it doesn’t prevent us from taking a tough position when such things as the ones we have seen in iran happen. it is the case for additional human rights listing in response to the protest. to tell the truth, there are currently no proposals for additional listing on the table. having said that, the process for the annual review of the human rights regime is ongoing. as things stand, we still have human rights sanctions in place, as i told you, which affect 83 individuals and entities in iran. this is a difficult question and we will continue working on it, at the same time using our diplomatic capacity in order to save a deal that is very important for our security and, on the other hand, defending human rights in iran and elsewhere with our sanctions regime, our diplomatic pressure and our permanent claim to respect human rights. this has been a very intensive part-session. it was my first part-session, ‘mon baptême de feu’. i would like to thank all of you for all your contributions and for your support. i know that not everybody sees things in the same way. from the left to the right, there are different approaches and different understandings of what’s happening in this difficult world in which we have to live. today, having covered four difficult issues, thank you all for your contribution and thank you for your support for my job. policy, and this includes the fight against fgm. we need to work with our international partners across the world to support the implementation of the un sustainable development goals, which cover violence against women. the european parliament has an important role to play through its political networks around the world. we have to acknowledge that fgm is a sensitive matter to tackle. it encompasses a variety of areas, from general human rights to women’s right to bodily integrity, the tackling of gender-based violence and the right to health. this is why it is important that we work together with fgm survivors, fgm practicing communities and experts in an empowering manner, free of misunderstandings and misconceptions. we must avoid stigmatisation, racism and xenophobia at all costs. we should instead promote empowerment, cooperation and dialogue. apart from funding and legislation, we must include all stakeholders at all levels, from our political dialogues with national administrations, to our support for health care professionals, religious and community leaders, and families in eradicating female genital mutilation once and for all. i look forward to your views today, and to working with you towards this mandate on this very important issue.
the commission is therefore proposing to amend the proposal to the effect that the commission should be able to take part in the discussions. the commission is not prepared to accept this. always, always, unfortunately, a woman's body is a battlefield. in every culture, in every religion, on every continent, at every time. it's always the female body that's being accessed. how do you explain that? we remember: it was the female bodies, for example in china, it was the women whose feet were bound so that they were crippled - the so-called lotus feet - it was not the men's feet. we remember: it was women's lacing -- corset lacing -- that deformed the body so that women had lifelong consequences, it wasn't men's bodies. and now in the present? now it's genital mutilation - a mutilation that robs women of sexuality, or partially robs women of sexuality, for life, that creates pain. and some of them are women sewn, they're not sewing men, they're sewing women. we have cultures too, cultures of virginity, for example, a culture of virgins, which is also emerging in europe. it's always, always, always about the same thing: it's about men's control over women's bodies, it's about shaping the body, it's about limitation, and it's about inflicting pain on women.
madam president, according to the world health organisation, it’s estimated that more than 200 million girls and women alive today have undergone female genital mutilation (fgm) in the countries where the practice occurs, and that includes eu member states. furthermore, there are an estimated three million girls at risk of being cut every year. fgm is a form of violence against women and girls. it’s not a defensible cultural practice, but another manifestation of patriarchy. we can and must end fgm, but we need to work with all sectors of society in order to educate families and communities, to empower women and girls to take control of their own bodies, and to encourage men to speak out about this brutal practice. today, i met an inspiring group of girl coders from kenya who were nominated for the sakharov prize for freedom of thought. they’ve created an award winning app called ‘i-cut’, which puts the power into the hands of those at risk by sign posting them to the nearest police station, health centre or ngo that can help them. these girls, and others like them, are leading the way.
madam president, commissioner, female genital mutilation is a plague that continues to threaten millions of women in some african countries and in europe. it is a clear violation of human rights, a denial of gender equality and an attack on children, who have a vaccine. it is made with training, cooperation, control and prevention. and europe must lead the way in using it to eradicate this nightmare from the catalogue of threats against women once and for all. our strength is the many laboratories that at the local and regional level manufacture antibodies for fathers, mothers, daughters, who are at risk of not being able to prevent or suffer this aggression when they travel to their home countries. there are care protocols in some regions, such as the basque country, which base a holistic strategy on the school and health centres dedicated to primary care. so education, health, social services and justice work in coordination. a network protects families, educates children and adults, prevents, cures and combats stigmatization. they also inspire cooperation measures outside europe, which take advantage of the knowledge acquired. the commission should also benefit from this experience.
madam president, female genital mutilation is an intolerable form of violence against women. it is practiced in childhood and causes physical harm, irreparable damage to physical and sexual health and emotional damage for life. this is a practice that particularly affects the most vulnerable: girls up to 15 years of age and the poorest classes. it is essential to criminalise and punish the perpetrators, but also to prevent this violence, to take action to raise awareness and to raise awareness in the communities where these crimes occur, to educate new generations about women's rights. in our view, the fight to eradicate female genital mutilation is inseparable from the fight to protect and strengthen women's sexual and reproductive rights, the need for access to education and, in particular, to sexual education and the improvement of maternal and child health conditions. ending female genital mutilation will be a step forward for the rights of women and girls, and that's why we fight.
the adoption of a european strategy to combat female genital mutilation in the world is essential. social conventions, religious traditions, stereotypes associated with female sexuality cannot continue to be a reason to cause irreparable harm to the bodies of girls and women. unfortunately, there is little reliable data available on this scourge that should no longer exist in the 21st century. it is known that there are about 200 million victims worldwide, 500,000 of them in the european union. there are therefore three aspects which we consider to be fundamental. firstly, that the member states collect more data on this phenomenon in their country and train their professionals to be more effective in combating it. secondly, that we support innovative technology-based responses such as the i-cut app by the group 'the restorers', the kenyan girls, sakharov prize finalists, who we are receiving here today and who, one day, i hope we can receive to receive the prize themselves, as prizewinners, and that we continue to advocate for the european union's accession to the istanbul convention, which provides for the criminalisation of female genital mutilation. we will not rest until one woman or girl is maimed.
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, the situation here discussed of the degrading bacha bazi custom in logar province, afghanistan, merits only the most vivid repudiation. under the guise of a so-called tradition, for too long we have turned a blind eye to a flagrant violation of children's rights and the most basic rules of human rights. the steps finally taken by the afghan government to eradicate this practice have proved insufficient and reports continue to arrive of children being abused and human rights defenders being attacked and arbitrarily detained. it is time to demand that all those who fight for the rights of children be released and protected and that this abuse be completely eradicated. that impunity be ended and that children be guaranteed access to justice and not be extradited when they seek refuge in other countries. we know that mindsets take time to change, and that's why we need the mobilization of afghan society, of activists, of the authorities, of the religious leaders. we all need to fight this scourge and it requires legislative change, but also intervention through education to protect future generations.
